<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/My_Story_(Gillard_book)" title="My Story (Gillard book)">My Story</a></i></li></ul>
<hr />
<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Government_of_Australia" title="Government of Australia"><img alt="Government of Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="31" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Julia_Gillard_sidebar" title="Template:Julia Gillard sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Julia_Gillard_sidebar" title="Template talk:Julia Gillard sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Julia_Gillard_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Julia Eileen Gillard</b> <span style="font-size:85%;"><span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a></span></span> (born 29 September 1961) is an Australian former politician who served as the 27th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a> from 2010 to 2013, holding office as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> (ALP). She previously served as the 13th <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Deputy Prime Minister of Australia">deputy prime minister of Australia</a> from 2007 to 2010, under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a>. She is the first and only female to hold either office in Australian history.
</p><p>Born in <a href="/wiki/Barry,_Vale_of_Glamorgan" title="Barry, Vale of Glamorgan">Barry</a>, Wales, Gillard migrated with her family to <a href="/wiki/Adelaide" title="Adelaide">Adelaide</a> in <a href="/wiki/South_Australia" title="South Australia">South Australia</a> in 1966. She attended <a href="/wiki/Mitcham_Primary_School" title="Mitcham Primary School">Mitcham Demonstration School</a> and <a href="/wiki/Unley_High_School" title="Unley High School">Unley High School</a>. Gillard went on to study at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Adelaide" title="University of Adelaide">University of Adelaide</a>, but switched to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">University of Melbourne</a> in 1982, where she graduated with a Bachelor of Laws in 1986 and a Bachelor of Arts in 1989. During this time, she was <a href="/wiki/Australian_Union_of_Students" title="Australian Union of Students">president of the Australian Union of Students</a> from 1983 to 1984. In 1987, Gillard joined the law firm <a href="/wiki/Slater_%26_Gordon" title="Slater &amp; Gordon">Slater &amp; Gordon</a>, eventually becoming a <a href="/wiki/Partner_(business_rank)" title="Partner (business rank)">partner</a> in 1990, specialising in industrial law. In 1996, she became chief of staff to <a href="/wiki/John_Brumby" title="John Brumby">John Brumby</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Victoria)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Victoria)">Leader of the Opposition in Victoria</a>.
</p><p>Gillard was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_federal_election" title="1998 Australian federal election">1998 election</a> for the seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Lalor" title="Division of Lalor">Lalor</a>. Following the <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 election</a>, she was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet of Australia">Shadow Cabinet</a>. In December 2006, Gillard became the running mate of Kevin Rudd in a successful <a href="/wiki/2006_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="2006 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">leadership challenge</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kim_Beazley" title="Kim Beazley">Kim Beazley</a>, becoming deputy leader of the opposition. After Labor's victory at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a>, she was appointed as the <a href="/wiki/Deputy_prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy prime minister of Australia">deputy prime minister of Australia</a>, and was also given the roles of <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Education_(Australia)" title="Minister for Education (Australia)">Minister for Education</a>, <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations (Australia)">Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Social_Inclusion" title="Minister for Social Inclusion">Minister for Social Inclusion</a>.
</p><p>On <span class="nowrap">24 June 2010</span>, after Rudd lost internal support within the Labor Party and resigned as leader, Gillard was elected unopposed as his replacement in a <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="2010 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">leadership spill</a>, and was sworn-in as prime minister. She led Labor through the <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election" title="2010 Australian federal election">2010 election</a> weeks later, which saw the first <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a> since 1940. Gillard was able to form a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> with the support of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Greens" title="Australian Greens">Greens</a> and three <a href="/wiki/Independent_(politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent (politician)">independents</a>. During its term of office, the <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a> introduced the <a href="/wiki/National_Disability_Insurance_Scheme" title="National Disability Insurance Scheme">National Disability Insurance Scheme</a> (NDIS), the <a href="/wiki/Gonski_Report" class="mw-redirect" title="Gonski Report">Gonski funding</a>, oversaw the initial rollout of the <a href="/wiki/National_Broadband_Network" title="National Broadband Network">National Broadband Network</a> (NBN), and controversially implemented a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_pricing_in_Australia" title="Carbon pricing in Australia">carbon pricing scheme</a>, which was widely perceived as a breach of a pre-election commitment. 
</p><p>Gillard's premiership was often undermined by party instability and numerous scandals, including the <a href="/wiki/AWU_affair" title="AWU affair">AWU affair</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Health_Services_Union_expenses_affair" title="Health Services Union expenses affair">Health Services Union expenses affair</a>. Gillard and Rudd became embroiled in a lengthy political rivalry, resulting in Gillard losing the leadership of the Labor Party back to him in a <a href="/wiki/June_2013_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill" title="June 2013 Australian Labor Party leadership spill">June 2013 leadership spill</a>. Her resignation as prime minister took effect the next day, and she announced her retirement from politics.
</p><p>In the years following her retirement, Gillard has been a <a href="/wiki/Visiting_scholar" title="Visiting scholar">visiting professor</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Adelaide" title="University of Adelaide">University of Adelaide</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Fellow" title="Fellow">Senior Fellow</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Brookings_Institution" title="Brookings Institution">Brookings Institution</a>'s Center for Universal Education, and has been the chair of the <a href="/wiki/Global_Partnership_for_Education" title="Global Partnership for Education">Global Partnership for Education</a> since 2014 and as the chair of <a href="/wiki/Beyond_Blue" title="Beyond Blue">Beyond Blue</a> since 2017. She released her memoir, <i><a href="/wiki/My_Story_(Gillard_book)" title="My Story (Gillard book)">My Story</a></i>, in September 2014. In April 2021, she became chair of the <a href="/wiki/Wellcome_Trust" title="Wellcome Trust">Wellcome Trust</a>, succeeding <a href="/wiki/Eliza_Manningham-Buller" title="Eliza Manningham-Buller">Eliza Manningham-Buller</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-wellcome1_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wellcome1-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Although Gillard often ranked poorly in opinion polls as prime minister,<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> her premiership has been more favourably received in retrospect.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Political experts often place her in the <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Australia">middle-to-upper tier</a> of Australian prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span></h3>
<p>Gillard was born on 29 September 1961 in <a href="/wiki/Barry,_Vale_of_Glamorgan" title="Barry, Vale of Glamorgan">Barry, Vale of Glamorgan</a>, Wales.<sup id="cite_ref-Biog_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Biog-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Julia_Gillard_comes_from_a_village_called_Cwmgwrach,_which_means_&#39;The_Valley_of_the_Witch&#39;_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Julia_Gillard_comes_from_a_village_called_Cwmgwrach,_which_means_&#39;The_Valley_of_the_Witch&#39;-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> She is the second of two daughters born to John Oliver Gillard (1929–2012) and the former Moira Mackenzie (born 1928); her older sister Alison was born in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-AusStory_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AusStory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Gillard's father was born in <a href="/wiki/Cwmgwrach" class="mw-redirect" title="Cwmgwrach">Cwmgwrach</a>, but was of predominantly English descent; he worked as a psychiatric nurse.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Her mother was born in Barry, and is of distant Scottish and Irish descent; she worked in a <a href="/wiki/Salvation_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Salvation Army">Salvation Army</a> nursing home.<sup id="cite_ref-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After Gillard suffered from <a href="/wiki/Bronchopneumonia" title="Bronchopneumonia">bronchopneumonia</a> as a child, her parents were advised it would aid her recovery if they were to live in a warmer climate.<sup id="cite_ref-AusStory_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AusStory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> This led the family to migrate to Australia in 1966, settling in <a href="/wiki/Adelaide" title="Adelaide">Adelaide</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Australia" title="South Australia">South Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-counterpoint_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-counterpoint-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The Gillard family's first month in Australia was spent in the <a href="/wiki/Migrant_hostels_of_South_Australia" title="Migrant hostels of South Australia">Pennington Hostel</a>, a now-closed migrant facility located in <a href="/wiki/Pennington,_South_Australia" title="Pennington, South Australia">Pennington</a>, South Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-penhos1_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-penhos1-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-penhos2_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-penhos2-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In 1974, eight years after they arrived, Gillard and her family became Australian citizens. As a result, Gillard held <a href="/wiki/Multiple_citizenship" title="Multiple citizenship">dual citizenship</a> until she <a href="/wiki/Renunciation_of_citizenship" title="Renunciation of citizenship">renounced</a> her British citizenship prior to entering the Australian parliament in 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_and_legal_career">Education and legal career</span></h3>
<p>Gillard attended <a href="/wiki/Mitcham_Primary_School" title="Mitcham Primary School">Mitcham Demonstration School</a> before going on to <a href="/wiki/Unley_High_School" title="Unley High School">Unley High School</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> She began an arts degree at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Adelaide" title="University of Adelaide">University of Adelaide</a>, during which she was president of the Adelaide University Union from 1981 to 1982.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In her second year at the university, Gillard was introduced to politics by the daughter of a state Labor minister.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (April 2018)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Accordingly, she joined the Labor Club and became involved in a campaign to fight federal education budget cuts.<sup id="cite_ref-AusStory_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AusStory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-counterpoint_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-counterpoint-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Gillard cut short her courses in Adelaide in 1982, and moved to Melbourne to work with the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Union_of_Students" title="Australian Union of Students">Australian Union of Students</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-roomatthetop_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-roomatthetop-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1983, she became the second woman to lead the Australian Union of Students, serving until the organisation's discontinuation in 1984. She was also the secretary of the left-wing organisation Socialist Forum.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-SocialistForum_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SocialistForum-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Having transferred her studies to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">University of Melbourne</a>, Gillard graduated with a <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws" title="Bachelor of Laws">Bachelor of Laws</a> degree in 1986 and a Bachelor of Arts degree in 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In 1987, she joined the law firm Slater &amp; Gordon in <a href="/wiki/Werribee,_Victoria" title="Werribee, Victoria">Werribee</a>, Victoria, working in <a href="/wiki/Industrial_law" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrial law">industrial law</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In 1990, she was admitted as a <a href="/wiki/Partner_(business_rank)" title="Partner (business rank)">partner</a>; at the age of 29, she was the youngest partner within the firm, and one of the first women to hold the position.<sup id="cite_ref-Davis_24_June_2010_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davis_24_June_2010-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-facesofjulia_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-facesofjulia-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_involvement">Early political involvement</span></h3>
<p>From 1985 to 1989, Gillard was president of the <a href="/wiki/Carlton,_Victoria" title="Carlton, Victoria">Carlton</a> branch of the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-Biog_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Biog-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> She stood for Labor <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Melbourne" title="Division of Melbourne">Division of Melbourne</a> prior to the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 federal election</a>, but was defeated by <a href="/wiki/Lindsay_Tanner" title="Lindsay Tanner">Lindsay Tanner</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-naa-bef_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-naa-bef-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 federal election</a>, Gillard won the third position on Labor's <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> ticket in Victoria, behind <a href="/wiki/Robert_Ray_(Australian_politician)" title="Robert Ray (Australian politician)">Robert Ray</a> and <a href="/wiki/Barney_Cooney" title="Barney Cooney">Barney Cooney</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-smhpreselection_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smhpreselection-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-smhpreselection2_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smhpreselection2-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> However, on the final distribution of preferences she was defeated by <a href="/wiki/Lyn_Allison" title="Lyn Allison">Lyn Allison</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Democrats" title="Australian Democrats">Australian Democrats</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Coorey1_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coorey1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1996, Gillard resigned from her position with Slater &amp; Gordon to serve as chief of staff to <a href="/wiki/John_Brumby" title="John Brumby">John Brumby</a>, at that time the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Victoria)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Victoria)">Leader of the Opposition in Victoria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Biog_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Biog-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Coorey1_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coorey1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> She was responsible for drafting the <a href="/wiki/Affirmative_action" title="Affirmative action">affirmative-action</a> rules within the Labor Party in Victoria that set the target of pre-selecting women for 35 per cent of "winnable seats". She also played a role in the foundation of <a href="/wiki/EMILY%27s_List_Australia" title="EMILY&#39;s List Australia">EMILY's List</a>, the pro-choice fund-raising and support network for Labor women.<sup id="cite_ref-Anne_Summers_250610_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Anne_Summers_250610-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard has cited Welsh <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> politician <a href="/wiki/Aneurin_Bevan" title="Aneurin Bevan">Aneurin Bevan</a> as one of her political heroes.<sup id="cite_ref-Davis_24_June_2010_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davis_24_June_2010-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Member_of_Parliament_.281998.E2.80.932007.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament_(1998–2007)">Member of Parliament (1998–2007)</span></h2>
<p>Gillard was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_federal_election" title="1998 Australian federal election">1998 federal election</a> representing <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Lalor" title="Division of Lalor">Lalor</a>, a safe Labor seat near Melbourne, replacing <a href="/wiki/Barry_Jones_(Australian_politician)" title="Barry Jones (Australian politician)">Barry Jones</a> who retired. She made her maiden speech to the House on 11 November 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Gillard was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives_committees#Standing_committees" title="Australian House of Representatives committees">standing committee</a> for Employment, Education and Workplace Relations from 8 December 1998 to 8 December 2001, in addition to Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Affairs from 20 March 2003 to 18 August 2003. Within the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives_committees#Joint_committees" title="Australian House of Representatives committees">joint committees</a>, she was a member of the Public Accounts and Audit from 8 December 1998 to 11 February 2002, in addition to the Native Title and the Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Land Fund from 20 March 2003 to 11 August 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-Biog_6-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Biog-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Shadow_Minister_.282001.E2.80.932007.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Shadow_Minister_(2001–2007)">Shadow Minister (2001–2007)</span></h3>
<p>After Labor's defeat at the <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001 federal election</a>, Gillard was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet of Australia">Shadow Cabinet</a> under then-Labor Leader <a href="/wiki/Simon_Crean" title="Simon Crean">Simon Crean</a>, where she was given responsibility for Population and Immigration. In February 2003, she was given additional responsibilities for Reconciliation and Indigenous Affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In these roles, in the wake of the <a href="/wiki/Tampa_affair" title="Tampa affair">Tampa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Children_Overboard_affair" title="Children Overboard affair">Children Overboard affairs</a>, which were partly credited with Labor's 2001 election loss, Gillard developed a new immigration policy for the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-counterpoint_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-counterpoint-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard was later promoted to the position of Shadow Minister for Health and Deputy Manager of Opposition Business in the House (to <a href="/wiki/Mark_Latham" title="Mark Latham">Mark Latham</a>) on 2 July 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-facesofjulia_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-facesofjulia-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> During this time, she shadowed <a href="/wiki/Tony_Abbott" title="Tony Abbott">Tony Abbott</a>, with the rivalry between the two often attracting attention from the media.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> She was later given additional responsibility for managing opposition business in the House of Representatives by Latham, who had succeeded Beazley as Labor Party leader.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG/220px-Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG/330px-Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG/440px-Kevin_Rudd_and_Julia_Gillard.JPG 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="518" /></a><figcaption>Gillard at her first press conference as Deputy Leader in 2006, alongside new Leader Kevin Rudd</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the aftermath of Labor's fourth consecutive defeat in the <a href="/wiki/2004_Australian_federal_election" title="2004 Australian federal election">2004 federal election</a> it was widely speculated that Gillard might challenge <a href="/wiki/Jenny_Macklin" title="Jenny Macklin">Jenny Macklin</a> for the deputy leadership, but she did not do so.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Gillard had been spoken of as a potential future leader of the party for some years, but never stood in a leadership contest. After Mark Latham resigned as Labor Leader in January 2005, Gillard appeared on <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">ABC</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Australian_Story" title="Australian Story">Australian Story</a></i> in March 2006, after which an Ipsos Mackay poll conducted for <a href="/wiki/Network_Ten" class="mw-redirect" title="Network Ten">Network Ten</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Meet_the_Press_(Australian_TV_program)" title="Meet the Press (Australian TV program)">Meet the Press</a></i> found that more respondents would prefer Gillard to be Labor Leader; she polled 32% compared with Beazley's 25% and Kevin Rudd's 18%.<sup id="cite_ref-AusStory_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AusStory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-The_Gillard_Diaries_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Gillard_Diaries-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Although she had significant cross-factional support, she announced on 25 January 2005 that she would not contest the leadership, allowing Beazley to be elected unopposed.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Deputy_Opposition_Leader_.282006.E2.80.932007.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Deputy_Opposition_Leader_(2006–2007)">Deputy Opposition Leader (2006–2007)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2006" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2006">Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2006</a></div>
<p>On 1 December 2006, as part of a cross-factional political partnership with Kevin Rudd, Gillard challenged Jenny Macklin for the deputy leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> After Rudd successfully replaced Beazley as Labor Leader on 4 December 2006, Macklin chose to resign, allowing Gillard to become Deputy Leader unopposed.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In the subsequent reshuffle, Gillard was allocated responsibility for Employment, Workplace Relations and Social Inclusion, as well as being made <a href="/wiki/List_of_Australian_Leaders_of_the_Opposition" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Australian Leaders of the Opposition">Deputy Leader of the Opposition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Deputy_Prime_Minister_.282007.E2.80.932010.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Deputy_Prime_Minister_(2007–2010)">Deputy Prime Minister (2007–2010)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Rudd_government_(2007%E2%80%9310)" class="mw-redirect" title="Rudd government (2007–10)">Rudd government (2007–10)</a></div>
<p>After the Labor Party's victory in the 2007 federal election, Gillard was sworn in as the first ever female Deputy Prime Minister of Australia on 3 December 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In addition to being appointed to the position of Deputy Prime Minister, Gillard was given responsibility for a so-called "super ministry", the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Education,_Employment_and_Workplace_Relations" title="Department of Education, Employment and Workplace Relations">Department of Education, Employment and Workplace Relations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg/220px-Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg/330px-Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg/440px-Julia_Gillard_US_Ambassador_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="427" /></a><figcaption>Gillard meets with <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Australia">US Ambassador</a> <a href="/wiki/Jeff_Bleich" title="Jeff Bleich">Jeff Bleich</a> on 26 November 2009</figcaption></figure>
<p>She was a member of the Strategic Priorities Budget Committee (SPBC) – also referred to as "<a href="/wiki/Gang_of_Four_(Australian_Labor_Party)" title="Gang of Four (Australian Labor Party)">Gang of Four</a>" – which comprised Rudd and his most senior ministers: Gillard, Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Swan" title="Wayne Swan">Wayne Swan</a> and Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Lindsay_Tanner" title="Lindsay Tanner">Lindsay Tanner</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Formed in late-2007 as a result of an internal review, the SPBC was responsible for the government's handling of the 2007–08 global <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%9308" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–08">financial crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 December 2007, Gillard was <a href="/wiki/Acting_prime_minister" title="Acting prime minister">acting prime minister</a> while Rudd attended the <a href="/wiki/2007_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference" title="2007 United Nations Climate Change Conference">United Nations Climate Change Conference</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bali" title="Bali">Bali</a>, becoming the first woman ever to hold that position.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC_10_December_2007_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC_10_December_2007-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> She assumed these duties for a total of 69 days throughout Rudd's tenure, during his various overseas travel engagements.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Gillard quickly became known as a highly regarded debater, with her performances during parliamentary question time prompting <a href="/wiki/Peter_van_Onselen" title="Peter van Onselen">Peter van Onselen</a> to call her "the best parliamentary performer on the Labor side".<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ministerial_portfolios">Ministerial portfolios</span></h3>
<p>In her role as Minister for Education, Gillard travelled to Washington D.C. in 2009, where she signed a deal with <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Education" title="United States Secretary of Education">US Secretary of Education</a> <a href="/wiki/Arne_Duncan" title="Arne Duncan">Arne Duncan</a> to encourage improved policy collaboration in education reform between both countries.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Curriculum,_Assessment_and_Reporting_Authority" title="Australian Curriculum, Assessment and Reporting Authority">Australian Curriculum, Assessment and Reporting Authority</a> (ACARA), an independent authority responsible for the development of a national curriculum, was amongst her first policy pursuits in 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> She launched the government's "<a href="/wiki/Digital_Education_Revolution" title="Digital Education Revolution">Digital Education Revolution</a>" (DER) program, which provided laptops to all public secondary school students and developed quality digital tools, resources and infrastructure for all schools.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In conjunction with DER, Gillard oversaw the "<a href="/wiki/Building_the_Education_Revolution" title="Building the Education Revolution">Building the Education Revolution</a>" (BER) program, which allocated <a href="/wiki/Australian_dollar" title="Australian dollar">$</a>16&#160;billion to build new school accommodation including classrooms, libraries and assembly halls.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard also ensured the implementation of the <a href="/wiki/National_Assessment_Program_%E2%80%93_Literacy_and_Numeracy" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assessment Program – Literacy and Numeracy">National Assessment Program – Literacy and Numeracy</a> (NAPLAN) in 2008, whereby a series of <a href="/wiki/Standardized_tests" class="mw-redirect" title="Standardized tests">standardised tests</a> focused on basic skills are administered annually to Australian students.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This was followed by the introduction of the <a href="/wiki/My_School" title="My School">My School</a> website; launched in January 2010, the website reports on data from NAPLAN and displays information such as school missions, staffing, financial information, its resources and its students' characteristics.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations, Gillard removed the <a href="/wiki/WorkChoices" title="WorkChoices">WorkChoices</a> industrial relations regime introduced by the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a>, and replaced it with the <i>Fair Work Act</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> This established a single industrial relations bureaucracy called <a href="/wiki/Fair_Work_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Fair Work Australia">Fair Work Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.282010.E2.80.932013.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(2010–2013)">Prime Minister (2010–2013)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_leadership_vote">2010 leadership vote</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2010" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2010">Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2010</a></div>
<p>Prime Minister Kevin Rudd suffered a decline in his personal ratings, and a perceived loss of support among his own MPs, following the failure of the Government's <a href="/wiki/Energy_Efficient_Homes_Package" title="Energy Efficient Homes Package">insulation program</a>, controversy regarding the implementation of a <a href="/wiki/Minerals_Resource_Rent_Tax" title="Minerals Resource Rent Tax">tax on mining</a>, the failure of the government to secure passage of its <a href="/wiki/Carbon_trading" class="mw-redirect" title="Carbon trading">carbon trading</a> scheme and some policy debate about immigration policy. Significant disaffection had arisen within the Labor Party as to the leadership style and direction of Rudd.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> On 23 June 2010 he announced that Gillard had asked him to hold a <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2010" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2010">leadership ballot</a> the following day to determine the leadership of the Labor Party, and hence the Prime Ministership of Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As late as May 2010, prior to challenging Rudd, Gillard was quipping to the media that "There's more chance of me becoming the <a href="/wiki/Full-forward" title="Full-forward">full-forward</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Western_Bulldogs" title="Western Bulldogs">Dogs</a> than there is of any change in the Labor Party".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, Gillard's move against Rudd on 23 June appeared to surprise many Labor backbenchers. <a href="/wiki/Daryl_Melham" title="Daryl Melham">Daryl Melham</a>, when asked by a reporter on the night of the challenge if indeed a challenge was on, replied: "Complete garbage. ABC have lost all credibility."<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> As he was being deposed, Rudd suggested that his opponents wanted to move Labor to the right, saying on 23 June: "This party and government will not be lurching to the right on the question of asylum seekers, as some have counselled us to do."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg/220px-Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="135" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg/330px-Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg/440px-Quentin_Bryce_swearing_in_Julia_Gillard_as_PM.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="307" /></a><figcaption>Gillard being sworn in as Prime Minister by <a href="/wiki/Quentin_Bryce" title="Quentin Bryce">Quentin Bryce</a> on 24 June 2010</figcaption></figure>
<p>Initially, <i>The Sydney Morning Herald</i> reported that the final catalyst for the move on Rudd was sparked by a report that Rudd had used his chief of staff to sound out back benchers on his level of support, thus implying that "he did not trust the repeated assurances by Ms Gillard that she would not stand".<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Later, ABC's 7:30 Report said the seeds for the challenge to Rudd came from "factional heavyweights" <a href="/wiki/Bill_Shorten" title="Bill Shorten">Bill Shorten</a> and Senator <a href="/wiki/David_Feeney" title="David Feeney">David Feeney</a>, who secured the support of "New South Wales right power broker" <a href="/wiki/Mark_Arbib" title="Mark Arbib">Mark Arbib</a> and that Feeney and Arbib went to discuss a challenge with Gillard on the morning of 23 June and a final numbers count began for a challenge.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Accounts have continued to differ as to the extent of Gillard's foreknowledge and planning of the replacement of Rudd.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rudd initially said that he would challenge Gillard, but it soon became apparent that he did not have enough support within the party to survive in his position. Hours before the vote on 24 June, he resigned as Prime Minister and Leader of the Labor Party, leaving Gillard to assume the leadership unopposed. <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> Wayne Swan was at the same time elected unopposed to succeed Gillard as Deputy Leader.<sup id="cite_ref-theaustralian1_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theaustralian1-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly afterward, Gillard was sworn in as the 27th Prime Minister of Australia by <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> <a href="/wiki/Quentin_Bryce" title="Quentin Bryce">Quentin Bryce</a>, with Swan being sworn in as Deputy Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The members of the <a href="/wiki/First_Rudd_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Rudd Ministry">Rudd Ministry</a>, with the exception of Rudd himself who returned to the backbenches, subsequently became the members of the <a href="/wiki/First_Gillard_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Gillard Ministry">First Gillard Ministry</a>. It was the first time in Australian history that both the head of state as well as the head of government were female.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Quentin_Bryce,_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg/220px-Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg/330px-Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg/440px-Quentin_Bryce%2C_Julia_Gillard_and_Wayne_Swan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="332" /></a><figcaption>Gillard alongside partner <a href="/wiki/Tim_Mathieson" title="Tim Mathieson">Tim Mathieson</a>, Quentin Bryce, <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Swan" title="Wayne Swan">Wayne Swan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Michael_Bryce" title="Michael Bryce">Michael Bryce</a> on 24 June 2010</figcaption></figure>
<p>Later that day, in her first press conference as Prime Minister, Gillard said that at times the <a href="/wiki/First_Rudd_government" class="mw-redirect" title="First Rudd government">Rudd government</a> "went off the tracks", and "[I] came to the view that a good Government was losing its way".<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Gillard offered wider explanation of her motivations for replacing Rudd during the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2012" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2012">2012 Labor leadership spill</a> in which Rudd challenged Gillard to regain the Labor leadership, telling the media that the Rudd government had entered a "period of paralysis" and that Rudd's work patterns were "difficult and chaotic".<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon her election by the Labor Party, Gillard said that she wouldn't move into <a href="/wiki/The_Lodge_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Lodge (Australia)">The Lodge</a> until she was elected Prime Minister in her own right, instead choosing to divide her time between a flat in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a> and her home in <a href="/wiki/Altona,_Victoria" title="Altona, Victoria">Altona</a>, a western suburb of Melbourne.<sup id="cite_ref-New_PM_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-New_PM-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Gillard moved into The Lodge on 26 September 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As well as being the first female Prime Minister, and the first never to have married, Gillard is the first Prime Minister since <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a> to have been born overseas.<sup id="cite_ref-theaustralian1_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theaustralian1-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The leadership question remained a feature of the <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a>'s terms in office, and amidst ongoing leadership speculation following an ABC TV <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Corners" title="Four Corners">Four Corners</a></i> examination of the events leading up to Rudd's replacement which cast doubt on Gillard's insistence that she did not actively campaign for the Prime Ministership, Attorney-General <a href="/wiki/Nicola_Roxon" title="Nicola Roxon">Nicola Roxon</a> spoke of Rudd's record in the following terms: "I don't think we should whitewash history – while there are a lot of very good things our government did with Kevin as prime minister, there were also a lot of challenges, and it's Julia who has seen through fixing a lot of those problems."<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_election">2010 election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election" title="2010 Australian federal election">2010 Australian federal election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg/220px-Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg/330px-Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg/440px-Leaders_of_TPP_member_states.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1776" data-file-height="888" /></a><figcaption>Gillard (3rd from left) attending a meeting of <a href="/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership" title="Trans-Pacific Partnership">Trans-Pacific Partnership</a> member state leaders</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 17 July 2010, 23 days after becoming prime minister and after receiving the agreement of the Governor-General Quentin Bryce, Gillard announced the next federal election would be held on 21 August 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Gillard began campaigning with a speech using the slogan "moving forward".<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In the early stages of the campaign, a series of leaks were released by purported Labor Party sources, indicating apparent divisions within Cabinet over the replacement of Kevin Rudd by Gillard.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Midway through the campaign, Gillard offered journalists a self-assessment of her campaign by saying that she had been paying too much attention to advisers in her strategy team, and she wanted to run a less "stage-managed" campaign:<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I think it's time for me to make sure that the real Julia is well and truly on display, so I'm going to step up and take personal charge of what we do in the campaign from this point.</p></blockquote>
<p>Gillard met Opposition leader Tony Abbott for one official debate during the campaign. Studio audience surveys by <a href="/wiki/Nine_Network" title="Nine Network">Channel Nine</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Network" title="Seven Network">Seven Network</a> suggested a win to Gillard.<sup id="cite_ref-archer1_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archer1-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Unable to agree on further debates, the leaders went on to appear separately on stage for questioning at community forums in Sydney and <a href="/wiki/Brisbane" title="Brisbane">Brisbane</a>, <a href="/wiki/Queensland" title="Queensland">Queensland</a>. An audience exit poll of the Rooty Hill RSL audience indicated an Abbott victory.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Gillard won the audience poll at the Broncos Leagues Club meeting in Brisbane on 18 August.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Gillard also appeared on the ABC's Q&amp;A program on 9 August.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> On 7 August, Gillard was questioned by former Labor leader turned Channel Nine reporter Mark Latham.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard officially "launched" Labor's campaign in Brisbane five days before polling day, outlining Labor policies and using the slogan: "Yes we will move forward together".<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Labor and the Coalition each won 72 seats in the 150-seat House of Representatives,<sup id="cite_ref-72all_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72all-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> four short of the requirement for <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>, resulting in the first hung parliament since the <a href="/wiki/1940_Australian_federal_election" title="1940 Australian federal election">1940 election</a>. Labor suffered an 11-seat swing, even though it won a bare majority of the two-party vote.<sup id="cite_ref-hung_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hung-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Both major party leaders sought to form a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bbc_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Six <a href="/wiki/Crossbencher" title="Crossbencher">crossbench</a> <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of Parliament">MPs</a> held the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_(parliament)" title="Balance of power (parliament)">balance of power</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Four crossbench MPs, Greens <a href="/wiki/Adam_Bandt" title="Adam Bandt">Adam Bandt</a> and independents <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Wilkie" title="Andrew Wilkie">Andrew Wilkie</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rob_Oakeshott" title="Rob Oakeshott">Rob Oakeshott</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tony_Windsor" title="Tony Windsor">Tony Windsor</a> declared their support for Labor on <a href="/wiki/Confidence_and_supply" title="Confidence and supply">confidence and supply</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3005179abc_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3005179abc-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> allowing Gillard and Labor to remain in power with a minority government.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-WaitFinallyOver_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WaitFinallyOver-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Governor-General Bryce swore in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Gillard_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Gillard Ministry">Second Gillard Ministry</a> on 14 September 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policies">Domestic policies</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span></h4>
<p>Gillard came to office in the aftermath of the global <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932008" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–2008">financial crisis of 2007–2008</a>. Government receipts fell during the international downturn and the Rudd government had employed pump priming expenditure.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Upon taking over as leader of the Labor Party on 24 June 2010, Gillard said she could "assure" Australians that the Federal Budget would be in surplus in 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-23_June_Press_Conference_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23_June_Press_Conference-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The Government continued to promise this outcome until December 2012. Gillard initially ruled out a "<a href="/wiki/Carbon_tax" title="Carbon tax">carbon tax</a>" but said that she would build community consensus for a price on carbon and open negotiations with the mining industry for a re-vamped mining profits tax.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23_June_Press_Conference_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23_June_Press_Conference-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Following the 2010 hung parliament election result, the Labor Party elected to adopt the Australian Greens preference for a carbon tax to transition to an emissions trading scheme, establishing a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_price" title="Carbon price">carbon price</a> via the <a href="/wiki/Clean_Energy_Act_2011" title="Clean Energy Act 2011">Clean Energy Act 2011</a>. The government also introduced a revised Minerals Resource Rent Tax and the <a href="/wiki/Flood_levy" title="Flood levy">Queensland Flood Levy</a>.
</p><p>The Gillard government stressed a need to return the Federal Budget to surplus for the 2012–13 financial year, and Gillard said that there would be "no ifs no buts" about this promise and that "failure is not an option here and we won't fail".<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated13_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated13-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In his <a href="/wiki/2012_Australian_federal_budget" title="2012 Australian federal budget">2012–13 Budget</a>, Treasurer Swan announced that the government would deliver a $1.5&#160;billion surplus.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated10_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated10-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> The government cut defence and foreign aid spending.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> In December 2012, Swan announced that the government no longer expected to achieve a surplus, citing falling revenue and global economic conditions.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span></h4>
<p>Like her predecessor Rudd, Gillard had said that health is a priority in her agenda. She announced during the 2010 election, that there would be an increase of 270 placements for emergency doctors and nurses and 3,000 extra nursing scholarships over the following 10 years.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> She also said <a href="/wiki/Mental_health" title="Mental health">mental health</a> would be a priority in her second term, with a $277&#160;million suicide-prevention package which would target high-risk groups.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> As the election delivered a hung parliament, a $1.8&#160;billion package was given to rural hospitals, which was agreed to by the independents to support her re-election.<sup id="cite_ref-3005179abc_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3005179abc-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2010, her government introduced legislation to reform funding arrangements for the health system, with the intention of giving the Commonwealth responsibility for providing the majority of funding to public hospitals and 100 per cent of funding for primary care and GP services.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> In February 2011, Gillard announced extensive revision of the original health funding reforms proposed by the Rudd government, which had been unable to secure the support of all state governments. The revised Gillard government plan proposed that the federal government move towards providing 50% of new health funding (and not 60 per cent as originally agreed) and removed the requirement of the states to cede a proportion of their <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Australia)">GST</a> revenue to the Federal Government to fund the new arrangement.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The new agreement was supported by all state premiers and chief ministers<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> and signed on 2 August.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span></h4>
<p>In relation to population targets for Australia, Gillard told <a href="/wiki/Fairfax_Media" title="Fairfax Media">Fairfax Media</a> in August 2010 that while skilled migration is important: "I don't support the idea of a <a href="/wiki/Big_Australia" title="Big Australia">big Australia</a>". Gillard also altered the nomenclature of <a href="/wiki/Tony_Burke" title="Tony Burke">Tony Burke</a>'s role as "Minister for Population" to that of "Minister for Sustainable Population".<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> The Government released a "sustainable population strategy" in May 2011 which did not specify a target population.<sup id="cite_ref-Even_Bigger_Australia_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Even_Bigger_Australia-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> In October 2011 trade minister <a href="/wiki/Craig_Emerson" title="Craig Emerson">Craig Emerson</a> released a paper with Gillard's approval which advocated for continued rapid rates of population growth.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg/220px-Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg/330px-Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg/440px-Julia_Gillard_speaking_at_the_National_Flag_Raising_and_Citizenship_ceremony.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3093" data-file-height="2322" /></a><figcaption>Gillard speaking at the National Flag Raising and Citizenship ceremony in Canberra, on 26 January 2013</figcaption></figure>
<p>After winning leadership of the Labor Party, Gillard identified addressing the issue of unauthorised arrivals of asylum seekers as a priority of her government. She announced that negotiations were underway for a return to "offshore processing" of asylum seeker claims. Gillard ruled out a return to processing at <a href="/wiki/Nauru" title="Nauru">Nauru</a> and named <a href="/wiki/East_Timor" title="East Timor">East Timor</a> as a preferred location for new detention and processing facilities.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> The East Timorese government rejected the plan.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2010, her government announced that it would open two detention centres for 2000 immigrants, due to the pressures in allowing women and children to be released into the community. One was to be opened in <a href="/wiki/Inverbrackie,_South_Australia" title="Inverbrackie, South Australia">Inverbrackie, South Australia</a>, and one in <a href="/wiki/Northam,_Western_Australia" title="Northam, Western Australia">Northam, Western Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> She said it would be a short-term solution to the problem and that temporary detention centres would be closed.
</p><p>On 15 December 2010, a ship containing 89 asylum seekers crashed on the shore of <a href="/wiki/Christmas_Island" title="Christmas Island">Christmas Island</a>, killing up to fifty people.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Refugee and migrant advocates condemned the government's hardline policy as responsible for the tragedy,<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> and Labor Party president <a href="/wiki/Anna_Bligh" title="Anna Bligh">Anna Bligh</a> called for a complete review of the party's asylum seeker policy.<sup id="cite_ref-xmasbligh_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-xmasbligh-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Gillard returned early from holidays in response to the crash, and to review asylum seeker policy.<sup id="cite_ref-xmasbligh_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-xmasbligh-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Some months later Gillard would announce "The Malaysia Solution" in response.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2011, Australia's federal government confirmed that a detention centre for single men would be built at the old army barracks at <a href="/wiki/Pontville" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontville">Pontville</a>, 45 minutes north of <a href="/wiki/Hobart" title="Hobart">Hobart</a>, Tasmania. This immigration detention centre would house up to 400 refugees.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Also in April 2011, immigration detainees at the Villawood detention centre rioted in protest of their treatment, setting fire to several buildings.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 2011, Gillard announced that Australia and <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a> were finalising an arrangement to exchange asylum seekers. Gillard and Immigration Minister <a href="/wiki/Chris_Bowen" title="Chris Bowen">Chris Bowen</a> said they were close to signing a bilateral agreement which would result in 800 asylum seekers who arrive in Australia by boat being taken to Malaysia instead. Australia would take 4,000 people from Malaysia who have previously been assessed as being refugees.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> However, on 31 August, the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court</a> ruled that the agreement to transfer refugees from Australia to Malaysia was invalid, and ordered that it not proceed. Australia would still accept 4,000 people who have been assessed as refugees in Malaysia.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The asylum seeker debate returned during August 2012 following the report of the Expert Panel on Asylum Seekers, led by retired <a href="/wiki/Air_Chief_Marshal_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Air Chief Marshal (Australia)">Air Chief Marshall</a> <a href="/wiki/Angus_Houston" title="Angus Houston">Angus Houston</a>. Accepting the panel's recommendation, Gillard on 12 August 2012 announced that a bill then before Parliament would be amended to allow the Government to choose sites for off-shore processing. At the same time she announced the Government would nominate the former detention centres on Nauru and <a href="/wiki/Manus_Island" title="Manus Island">Manus Island</a>, Papua New Guinea to be re-opened.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> The amended bill passed with the support of the Opposition on 16 August 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h4>
<p>Gillard held the responsibilities of the Education portfolio for four days after becoming Prime Minister, before appointing Simon Crean as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Education_(Australia)" title="Minister for Education (Australia)">Education Minister</a> on 28 June 2010. Following the 2010 election, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Garrett" title="Peter Garrett">Peter Garrett</a> assumed the role of Minister for Education, where he remained until June 2013. Gillard also altered the nomenclature of "Minister for Innovation, Industry, Science and Research" to comprise <a href="/wiki/Tertiary_education_in_Australia" title="Tertiary education in Australia">tertiary education</a>; <a href="/wiki/Chris_Evans_(Australian_politician)" title="Chris Evans (Australian politician)">Chris Evans</a>, Chris Bowen, and later, <a href="/wiki/Craig_Emerson" title="Craig Emerson">Craig Emerson</a>, each was <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Industry,_Innovation_and_Science_(Australia)#List_of_science_ministers" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Industry, Innovation and Science (Australia)">Minister for Tertiary Education, Skills, Science and Research</a> in the Gillard government.
</p><p>At the July 2010 National Press Club, Gillard stated "I will make education central to my economic agenda because of the role it plays in developing the skills that lead to rewarding and satisfying work – and that can build a high-productivity, high-participation economy."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> The Gillard government in January 2011 extended tax cuts to parents to help pay for stationery, textbooks or computer equipment under the Education Tax Refund scheme.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Education Minister under Rudd, Gillard commissioned <a href="/wiki/David_Gonski" title="David Gonski">David Gonski</a> to be chairman of a committee to make recommendations regarding funding of education in Australia. The findings and recommendations of the committee were later presented to the Gillard government in November 2011, whereafter deliberations were entered into by the Federal and state governments to consider its content. The committee's report is known as the <a href="/wiki/Gonski_Report" class="mw-redirect" title="Gonski Report">Gonski Report</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Subsequently, the proposed reforms (an increase in funding) became known as "Gonski" and supporters urged governments to "Give a Gonski". The report was removed from the government website by the newly elected <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a> after the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 Federal election</a> and is preserved by Australia's <a href="/wiki/Pandora_Archive" class="mw-redirect" title="Pandora Archive">Pandora Archive</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard continued to put the <a href="/wiki/My_School" title="My School">My School</a> website centre of her education agenda, which was controversial at the time when she implemented it as Minister for Education. Although it was popular amongst parents, the website helped parents view statistics of the school their children attended. She had since unveiled the revamped version, My School 2.0, promising better information to parents.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Universities also placed highly on her education agenda. Legislation which would have been voted on in November 2010 would have seen the introduction of a national universities regulator; however, this was delayed until 2011 following criticisms from the higher education sector. It was also announced by her government that legislation to establish the <a href="/wiki/Tertiary_Education_Quality_and_Standards_Agency" title="Tertiary Education Quality and Standards Agency">Tertiary Education Quality and Standards Agency</a> would also be introduced early 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate_change">Climate change</span></h4>
<p>The Rudd <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor</a> opposition promised to implement an <a href="/wiki/Emissions_trading_scheme" class="mw-redirect" title="Emissions trading scheme">emissions trading scheme</a> (ETS) before the 2007 federal election which Labor won. Rudd, unable to secure support for his scheme in the Senate, dropped it. During his 2012 leadership challenge against Gillard's prime ministership, Rudd said that it was Gillard and Swan who convinced him to delay his Emissions Trading Scheme.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 2010 election campaign, Gillard pledged to build a "national consensus" for a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_pricing_in_Australia" title="Carbon pricing in Australia">carbon price</a> by creating a "citizens assembly", to examine "the evidence on <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Australia" title="Climate change in Australia">climate change</a>, the case for action and the possible consequences of introducing a market-based approach to limiting and reducing <a href="/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_by_Australia" title="Greenhouse gas emissions by Australia">carbon emissions</a>", over the course of one year. The assembly was to be selected by an independent authority who would select people from the electoral roll using census data.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> The plan was never implemented. After the 2010 Election, Gillard agreed to form a minority government with the Greens and Independents and replaced her "citizens assembly" plan with a climate change panel consisting of Labor, Greens and independent members of the Australian parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> The panel ultimately announced backing for a temporary carbon tax, leading up to an Emissions Trading Scheme.
</p><p>During the 2010 election campaign, Gillard also said that no carbon tax would be introduced under a government she led.<sup id="cite_ref-NoCarbonTax_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NoCarbonTax-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> In the first hung parliament result in 70 years, the Gillard government, with the support of the Australian Greens and some cross bench independents, negotiated the implementation of a carbon tax (the preferred policy of the Australian Greens), by which a fixed-price carbon tax would proceed to a floating-price ETS within a few years under the plans. The government proposed the Clean Energy Bill in February 2011,<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> which the opposition claimed to be a broken election promise.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The bill was passed by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Lower House</a> in October 2011<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Upper House</a> in November 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Poker_machines">Poker machines</span></h4>
<p>In 2010, Gillard agreed with <a href="/wiki/Nick_Xenophon" title="Nick Xenophon">Nick Xenophon</a>, Andrew Wilkie and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Greens" title="Australian Greens">Australian Greens</a> to introduce <a href="/wiki/Poker_machine" class="mw-redirect" title="Poker machine">poker machine</a> reform legislation (to curb problem gambling) into the Australian parliament by May 2012. After members of the <a href="/wiki/Cross_bench" class="mw-redirect" title="Cross bench">cross bench</a> advised that they would not support this bill in the Australian House of Representatives, Gillard withdrew her support. Wilkie said that many Australians felt "very let down by the PM", and fellow anti-gambling campaigner Xenophon accused the Prime Minister of "backstabbing the person who put her in office".<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 January 2012, Wilkie announced that he was withdrawing his support for the Gillard government after it broke the agreement he had signed with Gillard to implement mandatory <a href="/wiki/Precommitment" title="Precommitment">precommitment</a> for all poker machines by 2014. He stated that he would support the government's alternative plan to trial pre-commitment in the ACT and require that pre-commitment technology be installed in all poker machines built from 2013, but that this fell short of what he had been promised in return for supporting the government.<sup id="cite_ref-Wilkie_withdraws_support_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilkie_withdraws_support-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> In response, Gillard and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Families,_Housing,_Community_Services_and_Indigenous_Affairs_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Families, Housing, Community Services and Indigenous Affairs (Australia)">Minister for Families, Housing, Community Services and Indigenous Affairs</a> Jenny Macklin argued that there was not enough support in the House of Representatives for Wilkie's preferred option for it to be passed, and that they had been advised it was technically unfeasible to implement mandatory commitment within the time frame he had specified.<sup id="cite_ref-PM_unveils_compromise_deal_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PM_unveils_compromise_deal-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Same-sex_marriage">Same-sex marriage</span></h4>
