<p>The <a href="/wiki/2011_Australian_Labor_Party_National_Conference" title="2011 Australian Labor Party National Conference">triennial Labor conference</a> held in December 2011 saw Gillard successfully negotiate an amendment on same-sex marriage to see the party introduce a <a href="/wiki/Conscience_vote" title="Conscience vote">conscience vote</a> to parliament through a <a href="/wiki/Private_member%27s_bill" title="Private member&#39;s bill">private member's bill</a>, rather than a binding vote.<sup id="cite_ref-tacticalswitch_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tacticalswitch-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Despite Gillard, who had previously stated her personal objection to same-sex marriage, the motion passed narrowly by 208 votes to 184.<sup id="cite_ref-ssmpassed_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ssmpassed-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ssmpassed2_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ssmpassed2-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> In February 2012, two bills to allow same-sex marriage in Australia were introduced in the 43rd Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 19 September 2012, the House of Representatives voted against passing its same-sex marriage bill by a margin of 98–42 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> On 21 September 2012, the Senate also voted down its same-sex marriage legislation, by a vote of 41–26.<sup id="cite_ref-samesexdownabc_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-samesexdownabc-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Forced_adoptions">Forced adoptions</span></h4>
<p>On 21 March 2013, Gillard delivered a national apology on behalf of the Australian Parliament to all those affected by the <a href="/wiki/Forced_adoption_in_Australia" title="Forced adoption in Australia">forced adoption practices</a> that took place in Australia from the late–1950s to the 1970s.<sup id="cite_ref-Apology1_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apology1-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> The apology, held in the Great Hall of <a href="/wiki/Parliament_House,_Canberra" title="Parliament House, Canberra">Parliament House</a>, was well–received by the 800 attendees, most of whom were victims or shared a connection to these practices.<sup id="cite_ref-Apology1_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apology1-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Gillard opened her speech by announcing that the Parliament would take responsibility for the practice of forced adoptions:<sup id="cite_ref-Apology2_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apology2-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Today, this Parliament, on behalf of the Australian people, takes responsibility and apologises for the policies and practices that forced the separation of mothers from their babies which created a lifelong legacy of pain and suffering.</p></blockquote>
<p>In the speech, Gillard committed to $5&#160;million worth of specialist support and records tracing for victims of forced adoptions, and an additional $1.5&#160;million towards the <a href="/wiki/National_Archives_of_Australia" title="National Archives of Australia">National Archives of Australia</a> "to record the experiences of those affected by forced adoption through a special exhibition."<sup id="cite_ref-Apologytranscript_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apologytranscript-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Commonwealth">Commonwealth</span></h3>
<p>Gillard represented Australia at the <a href="/wiki/Wedding_of_Prince_William_and_Catherine_Middleton" title="Wedding of Prince William and Catherine Middleton">Wedding of Prince William and Catherine Middleton</a> in London in April 2011 and hosted the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Heads_of_Government_Meeting" title="Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting">Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting</a> (CHOGM) in Perth in October of that year.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Heads_of_Government_Meeting_2011" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting 2011">Perth CHOGM</a> saw the historic announcement, by Gillard and British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, of changes to the succession laws regarding to thrones of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_realm" title="Commonwealth realm">Commonwealth realms</a>, overturning rules privileging male over female heirs to the line of succession and removing a ban on Roman Catholic consorts.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> At the CHOGM, Gillard also hosted <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a>, <a href="/wiki/Queen_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen of Australia">Queen of Australia</a> in what was almost certainly the monarch's final tour Down Under, due to her advanced age.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_(5510083265).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg/220px-Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg/330px-Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg/440px-Secretary_Clinton_Shakes_Hands_With_Australian_Prime_Minister_Gillard_%285510083265%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1331" /></a><figcaption>U.S. Secretary of State Hillary Clinton shakes hands with Gillard, 8 March 2011</figcaption></figure>
<p>During her first major international tour as prime minister in late 2010, Gillard told ABC TV's <i><a href="/wiki/The_7.30_Report" title="The 7.30 Report">7.30 Report</a></i>:<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Foreign policy is not my passion. It's not what I've spent my life doing. You know, I came into politics predominantly to make a difference to opportunity questions, particularly make a difference in education. So, yes, if I had a choice I'd probably more be in a school watching kids learn to read in Australia than here in Brussels at international meetings.</p></blockquote>
<p>When Gillard replaced Rudd in 2010, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Smith_(Australian_politician)" title="Stephen Smith (Australian politician)">Stephen Smith</a> retained the portfolio of Foreign Affairs up until the 2010 election, when he was moved to Defence. Following her 2010 election victory, Gillard selected her former leader Kevin Rudd (a career diplomat) as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_(Australia)" title="Minister for Foreign Affairs (Australia)">Foreign Minister</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> After Rudd's unsuccessful <a href="#2012_leadership_vote">leadership challenge</a> in February 2012, Gillard appointed <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a> to succeed Rudd as Foreign Affairs Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> When Gillard was not present in the Australia due to international commitments, or in other circumstances, Wayne Swan assumed the title of <a href="/wiki/Acting_prime_minister" title="Acting prime minister">acting prime minister</a>; when neither leader nor deputy were present in Australia, <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Government_in_the_Senate_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Government in the Senate (Australia)">Leader of the Government in the Senate</a> <a href="/wiki/Chris_Evans_(Australian_politician)" title="Chris Evans (Australian politician)">Chris Evans</a> assumed the role, as occurred in October and November 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the creation of a no-fly zone, which Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> vocally supported, Gillard voiced strong support for the <a href="/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya" title="2011 military intervention in Libya">2011 military intervention in Libya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Gillard government released the <i>Asian Century White Paper</i> in October 2012, offering a strategic framework for "Australia's navigation of the Asian Century". The report included focus on Australia's relations with China, <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a>, the key <a href="/wiki/Association_of_Southeast_Asian_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="Association of Southeast Asian Nations">ASEAN</a> countries as well as Japan and <a href="/wiki/South_Korea" title="South Korea">South Korea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 19 October 2012, Australia <a href="/wiki/2012_United_Nations_Security_Council_election" title="2012 United Nations Security Council election">secured election</a> to a seat as a <a href="/wiki/UN_Security_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="UN Security Council">Non-Permanent Member of the United Nations Security Council</a>. The initiative had been launched by the Rudd government, and further pursued under the Gillard government.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Afghanistan">Afghanistan</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg/220px-Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg/330px-Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg/440px-Julia_Gillard_with_Gen_David_H_Petraeus_Oct_2010.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3600" data-file-height="2400" /></a><figcaption>Gillard with General <a href="/wiki/David_Petraeus" title="David Petraeus">David Petraeus</a>, the commander of the <a href="/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force" title="International Security Assistance Force">International Security Assistance Force</a>, during a visit to Afghanistan on 2 October 2010</figcaption></figure>
<p>On her first day as prime minister, Gillard reassured US president <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> of Australia's continuing support for the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%93present)" class="mw-redirect" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–present)">military campaign</a> in Afghanistan, which was then in its ninth year of operation.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She visited Afghanistan on 2 October 2010, meeting with members of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Defence_Force" title="Australian Defence Force">Australian Defence Force</a> in <a href="/wiki/Tarinkot" title="Tarinkot">Tarinkot</a>, and President <a href="/wiki/Hamid_Karzai" title="Hamid Karzai">Hamid Karzai</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kabul" title="Kabul">Kabul</a>. The visit marked her first foreign trip as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Following the visit, A parliamentary debate was conducted for four sitting weeks of parliament in November 2010, with the agreement between Gillard and Abbott that it would be necessary for Australian soldiers to stay in Afghanistan and prevent it from becoming a safe haven for terrorists.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> She made her second trip to Afghanistan on 7 November 2011; much like her first trip, Gillard visited the 1,550 Australian troops based in Tarinkot, before meeting Karzai in Kabul where the two discussed the transition plans for Afghan military control. Whilst in Kabul, she opened Australia's newest <a href="/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_Australia#Asia" title="List of diplomatic missions of Australia">embassy</a> in Afghanistan.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2012, Gillard announced at a speech to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Strategic_Policy_Institute" title="Australian Strategic Policy Institute">Australian Strategic Policy Institute</a> that her government would withdraw all Australian combat forces from Afghanistan by the end of 2013, a year earlier than anticipated; nevertheless, she also committed Australia to long-term military and financial support for Afghanistan in the years following the 2014 transition to military control.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> Gillard made her third and final trip to the country on 15 October 2012, where she met with President Kurzai, the governor of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Urozgan" class="mw-redirect" title="List of governors of Urozgan">Urozgan</a> Province, before visiting the troops based in the aforementioned province.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="India">India</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Prime_Minister_of_Australia,_Ms._Julia_Gillard_being_received_by_the_Minister_of_State_for_Communications_and_Information_Technology,_Shri_Sachin_Pilot,_at_Air_Force_Station,_Palam,_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15,_2012.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Prime_Minister_of_Australia%2C_Ms._Julia_Gillard_being_received_by_the_Minister_of_State_for_Communications_and_Information_Technology%2C_Shri_Sachin_Pilot%2C_at_Air_Force_Station%2C_Palam%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2012.jpg/170px-thumbnail.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="197" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Prime_Minister_of_Australia%2C_Ms._Julia_Gillard_being_received_by_the_Minister_of_State_for_Communications_and_Information_Technology%2C_Shri_Sachin_Pilot%2C_at_Air_Force_Station%2C_Palam%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2012.jpg/255px-thumbnail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/The_Prime_Minister_of_Australia%2C_Ms._Julia_Gillard_being_received_by_the_Minister_of_State_for_Communications_and_Information_Technology%2C_Shri_Sachin_Pilot%2C_at_Air_Force_Station%2C_Palam%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_October_15%2C_2012.jpg/340px-thumbnail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1473" data-file-height="1710" /></a><figcaption>Gillard welcomed by the Minister of State for Communications and Information Technology, Shri Sachin Pilot, in <a href="/wiki/New_Delhi" title="New Delhi">New Delhi</a> on 15 October 2012</figcaption></figure>
<p>Relations between <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93India_relations" title="Australia–India relations">Australia and India</a> improved throughout Gillard's premiership, following a strained period between the two countries as a result of the Rudd government's decision to ban <a href="/wiki/Uranium" title="Uranium">uranium</a> sales to India in 2007, and the prolonged attacks against <a href="/wiki/Indian_Australians" title="Indian Australians">Indians living in Australia</a> during 2009 to 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2011, Gillard announced a desire to allow uranium exports to India, as a matter of "national interest, a decision about strengthening our strategic partnership with India in this the Asian century."<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_News_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_News-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The Rudd government had previously blocked uranium sales to India as a result of the Indian Government not being a signatory of the <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty">Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> The change in policy was supported a month later at the Labor Party National Conference, and Gillard reversed Australia's ban on exporting uranium to India on 4 December 2011. Gillard further expressed that any future agreement to sell uranium to India would include strict safeguards to ensure it would only be used for civilian purposes, and not end up in nuclear weapons.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_News_183-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_News-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard made her prime-ministerial visit to India on 16 October 2012, for a three-day bilateral meeting with Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Manmohan_Singh" title="Manmohan Singh">Manmohan Singh</a>, where they negotiated the safeguards required prior to the commencement of uranium trading between India and Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> The prospect of a quick trading arrangement was downplayed by both leaders in 2012;<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> nevertheless, Gillard's efforts in brokering the deal was a precursor of the agreement being finalised between Prime Minister Tony Abbott and his Indian counterpart, <a href="/wiki/Narendra_Modi" title="Narendra Modi">Narendra Modi</a>, in 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> This was her second trip to India whilst in Government; on 31 August 2009, Gillard, then–deputy prime minister, met in India with Minister of Human Resource Development <a href="/wiki/Kapil_Sibal" title="Kapil Sibal">Kapil Sibal</a> for the purpose of discussing the Australian Government's response to the string of attacks on Indian people living in Australia and attending Australian educational institutions.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Zealand">New Zealand</span></h4>
<p>Gillard maintained the close bonds between <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations" title="Australia–New Zealand relations">Australia and New Zealand</a> throughout her tenure as prime minister. She had a close working relationship with her New Zealand counterpart, Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Key" title="John Key">John Key</a>, who was among the first international leaders to congratulate Gillard on gaining the premiership in June 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In late 2010, the <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">World Trade Organization</a> overturned Australia's 1921 import restriction on New Zealand apples on the basis such ban was 'unscientific', after the New Zealand Government had appealed against a decision by the Rudd government which imposed further quarantine measures.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Gillard and Key had previously made a symbolic bet on the outcome of the <a href="/wiki/2011_Rugby_World_Cup" title="2011 Rugby World Cup">2011 Rugby World Cup</a> held in New Zealand, whereby the losing team of either leader would eat an apple of whichever of the two countries won; New Zealand won, and Gillard would later honour the bet in February 2013, during a dinner with Key.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 15 February 2011, Gillard made her first trip to New Zealand, during which she met with Key and held a luncheon with business leaders in <a href="/wiki/Auckland" title="Auckland">Auckland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> It marked the first New Zealand visit of a prime minister since Howard visited in 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> To conclude her two-day visit to New Zealand, Gillard travelled to <a href="/wiki/Wellington" title="Wellington">Wellington</a> on 16 February, where she became the first foreign dignitary to address the <a href="/wiki/49th_New_Zealand_Parliament" title="49th New Zealand Parliament">New Zealand Parliament</a> in its history.<sup id="cite_ref-speechinnz_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-speechinnz-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> In her speech, Gillard reflected on the countries' close ties to one another, their shared defence history, and efforts to increase economic cooperation.<sup id="cite_ref-speechinnz_199-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-speechinnz-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Her second visit to New Zealand, coincided with the September 2011 gathering of the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum" title="Pacific Islands Forum">Pacific Islands Forum</a>, held in Auckland, of which both Australia and New Zealand are members.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Gillard made her final trip to New Zealand on 9 February 2013; visiting <a href="/wiki/Queenstown,_New_Zealand" title="Queenstown, New Zealand">Queenstown</a>, she and Prime Minister Key announced a deal on asylum seekers, which would see New Zealand accept 150 refugees annually from Australia, starting in 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="United_States">United States</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><span><video id="mwe_player_0" poster="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv/220px--Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv.jpg" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="220" height="165" data-durationhint="219" data-mwtitle="Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons" resource="/wiki/File:Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/17/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv.480p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm; codecs=&quot;vp8, vorbis&quot;" data-title="SD WebM (480P)" data-shorttitle="WebM 480P" data-transcodekey="480p.webm" data-width="640" data-height="480" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/17/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv" type="video/ogg; codecs=&quot;vp9, opus&quot;" data-title="Low bandwidth VP9 (180P)" data-shorttitle="VP9 180P" data-transcodekey="180p.vp9.webm" data-width="240" data-height="180" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/17/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv/Australian_PM_Julia_Gillard_addresses_US_Congress_2011_snippet.ogv.240p.vp9.webm" type="video/webm;
