<p>In a 2008 speech in Washington, Gillard endorsed the <a href="/wiki/ANZUS" title="ANZUS">ANZUS</a> Alliance and described the United States as a civilising global influence.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Her former colleague and leader Mark Latham wrote in a 2009 article for the <i><a href="/wiki/Australian_Financial_Review" title="Australian Financial Review">Australian Financial Review</a></i> that these comments were "hypocritical", given past private communications Gillard had exchanged with him which apparently mocked elements of American foreign policy: "One of them concerned her study tour of the US, sponsored by the American Government in 2006—or to use her moniker—'a CIA re-education course'. She asked me to 'stand by for emails explaining <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George Bush</a> is a great statesman, torture is justified in many circumstances and those Iraqi insurgents should just get over it'."<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 9 March 2011, Gillard travelled to the United States to mark the 60th Anniversary of the ANZUS Alliance. She held formal meetings with President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>, Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Hillary_Clinton" title="Hillary Clinton">Hillary Clinton</a>, Treasury Secretary <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Geithner" title="Timothy Geithner">Timothy Geithner</a>, and UN Secretary-General <a href="/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon" title="Ban Ki-moon">Ban Ki-moon</a>. She also met with First Lady <a href="/wiki/Michelle_Obama" title="Michelle Obama">Michelle Obama</a>, and senior US Senator <a href="/wiki/John_McCain" title="John McCain">John McCain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Gillard addressed a joint session of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">United States Congress</a>, becoming the fourth Australian leader to do so and first foreign dignitary to address the 112th congress.<sup id="cite_ref-www.smh.com.au_gillard-pushes-the-right-buttons-as-she-woos-the-us-20110310-1bob4_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-www.smh.com.au_gillard-pushes-the-right-buttons-as-she-woos-the-us-20110310-1bob4-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> In her speech to Congress, Gillard reiterated Australia's diplomatic and security alliance with the United States, and noted that the United States has "a true friend <a href="/wiki/Down_under" class="mw-redirect" title="Down under">down under</a>&#160;... In both our countries, true friends stick together – in both our countries real mates talk straight&#160;... So as a friend I urge you only this – be worthy to your best traditions. Be bold."<sup id="cite_ref-www.smh.com.au_gillard-pushes-the-right-buttons-as-she-woos-the-us-20110310-1bob4_206-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-www.smh.com.au_gillard-pushes-the-right-buttons-as-she-woos-the-us-20110310-1bob4-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gender_politics">Gender politics</span></h3>
<p>During the course of Gillard's prime ministership, <a href="/wiki/Sexism" title="Sexism">sexism</a> had been a contentious issue for a number of Labor and Greens Party figures, as well as some commentators.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Former Labor Party advisor <a href="/wiki/Anne_Summers" title="Anne Summers">Anne Summers</a> said in 2012 that "Gillard is being persecuted both because she is a woman and in ways that would be impossible to apply to a man".<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> In reply, journalist <a href="/wiki/Peter_Hartcher" title="Peter Hartcher">Peter Hartcher</a> wrote, "She was a woman when she was popular; she can't be unpopular now because she's a woman. The change is a result of her actions in office, not her gender."<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Misogyny_speech">Misogyny speech</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Misogyny_Speech" title="Misogyny Speech">Misogyny Speech</a></div>
<p>In an August 2012 press conference regarding the <a href="/wiki/AWU_affair" title="AWU affair">AWU affair</a>, Gillard was critical of <i>The Australian</i> newspaper for writing about her connection to the affair and of what she called "misogynist nut jobs on the internet". Gillard said that she had been "the subject of a very sexist smear campaign".<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> In early October, the Opposition Leader's wife, <a href="/wiki/Margie_Abbott" title="Margie Abbott">Margie Abbott</a>, accused the Gillard government of a deliberate campaign to smear Tony Abbott, on gender issues.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 9 October 2012, Gillard also raised "sexism and misogyny" in a <a href="/wiki/Misogyny_speech" class="mw-redirect" title="Misogyny speech">speech</a> opposing a motion to remove <a href="/wiki/Peter_Slipper" title="Peter Slipper">Peter Slipper</a>, her choice as Speaker of the House of Representatives, after revelations of inappropriate conduct on his part became public.<sup id="cite_ref-NewYorker20121009_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NewYorker20121009-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Gillard linked the speech to the context of the then ongoing <a href="/wiki/Alan_Jones_%22died_of_shame%22_controversy" title="Alan Jones &quot;died of shame&quot; controversy">Alan Jones "died of shame" controversy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-NYT20121011_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NYT20121011-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> The speech<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> was widely reported around the world.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> In Laos soon after for an Asian-European leaders conference, Gillard described comments by <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande" title="François Hollande">François Hollande</a> and <a href="/wiki/Helle_Thorning-Schmidt" title="Helle Thorning-Schmidt">Helle Thorning-Schmidt</a>: "The president of France congratulated me on the speech, as did the Prime Minister of Denmark, and some other leaders, just casually as I've moved around, have also mentioned it to me."<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Farr,_Malcolm_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farr,_Malcolm-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> US president <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> reportedly "complimented" Gillard on the speech in a private conversation following his re-election,<sup id="cite_ref-Farr,_Malcolm_218-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farr,_Malcolm-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> and his Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Hillary_Clinton" title="Hillary Clinton">Hillary Clinton</a> praised the speech as "very striking" with Gillard going "chapter and verse".<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Labor had secured the defection of Slipper from the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_National_Party_of_Queensland" title="Liberal National Party of Queensland">Liberal National Party of Queensland</a> (LNP) to sit in the Speaker's chair a year earlier, but he was forced to stand aside from his main duties in April 2012 pending the conclusion of a criminal investigation.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> After a week of controversy, Gillard announced that she was asking Slipper to delay his return to the chair pending the conclusion of concurrent civil proceedings, in an effort to dispel what she described as a "dark cloud" over her government (a reference also to the ongoing <a href="/wiki/Craig_Thomson_affair" class="mw-redirect" title="Craig Thomson affair">Craig Thomson affair</a> involving a Labor MP linked to corruption allegations).<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2012_leadership_vote">2012 leadership vote</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2012" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2012">Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2012</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_2012_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Julia_Gillard_2012_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-Julia_Gillard_2012_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fd/Julia_Gillard_2012_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="237" data-file-height="326" /></a><figcaption>Gillard in 2012</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the light of poor polling results for the Gillard government, speculation that Foreign Minister and former Prime Minister Kevin Rudd wished to challenge Gillard for the leadership culminated with Rudd resigning from the Cabinet on 22 February 2012. Rudd told the media "I can only serve as Foreign Minister if I have the confidence of Prime Minister Gillard and her senior ministers" after Gillard failed to repudiate cabinet ministers who publicly criticised Rudd and his tenure as Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> The situation had been further exacerbated by the revelation on <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Corners_(Australian_TV_program)" title="Four Corners (Australian TV program)">Four Corners</a></i> that Gillard's staff wrote her victory speech for the 2010 leadership election two weeks prior to her challenge, contradicting Gillard's earlier claims that she had only resolved to challenge Rudd the day before the vote. This revelation caused particular conflict between Labor factions to surface, with Labor MP <a href="/wiki/Darren_Cheeseman" title="Darren Cheeseman">Darren Cheeseman</a> calling on Gillard to resign, while his colleague <a href="/wiki/Steve_Gibbons_(politician)" title="Steve Gibbons (politician)">Steve Gibbons</a> called Rudd a "psychopath with a giant ego".<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After resigning, Rudd stated that he did not think Gillard could defeat the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Coalition</a> at the next election and that, since his resignation, he had received encouragement from Labor MPs and Cabinet Ministers to contest the leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Gillard responded to these developments by announcing a leadership ballot for the morning of 27 February 2012, saying that if she lost the vote she would return to the <a href="/wiki/Backbench" class="mw-redirect" title="Backbench">backbench</a> and renounce any claims to the leadership. She asked that Rudd make the same commitment.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the leadership ballot, Gillard won comfortably by a vote of 71 to 31.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="March_2013_leadership_vote">March 2013 leadership vote</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_March_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, March 2013">Australian Labor Party leadership spill, March 2013</a></div>
<p>Despite Gillard's defeating Kevin Rudd comfortably in the 2012 leadership spill, tensions remained in the Labor Party regarding Gillard's leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> After Labor's polling position worsened in the wake of Gillard announcing the date of the 2013 election, these tensions came to a head when former Labor Leader and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Regional_Australia,_Regional_Development_and_Local_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Regional Australia, Regional Development and Local Government">Regional Minister</a> Simon Crean called for a leadership spill and backed Rudd on 21 March 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> In response, Gillard sacked Crean from his position, and called a leadership spill for 4.30&#160;pm that same day.
</p><p>Ten minutes before the ballot was due to occur, Rudd publicly announced that he would not contest the leadership, in line with the commitment he had made following the 2012 contest. As such, Gillard and Wayne Swan were the only candidates for the Leadership and Deputy Leadership of the Labor Party, and were elected unopposed. This marked the first time in history that an incumbent Labor Leader was elected unopposed at a leadership ballot.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Several ministers subsequently resigned from the government, including Chief Government Whip <a href="/wiki/Joel_Fitzgibbon" title="Joel Fitzgibbon">Joel Fitzgibbon</a>, Human Services Minister <a href="/wiki/Kim_Carr" title="Kim Carr">Kim Carr</a>, and Energy Minister <a href="/wiki/Martin_Ferguson_(politician)" title="Martin Ferguson (politician)">Martin Ferguson</a>.
</p><p>Gillard declared that the question of the Labor leadership was now "settled". Nevertheless, speculation on Gillard's leadership remained a major issue, with polling results indicating an electoral disaster were she to lead the Labor Party into the election. In light of this, media attention once more turned to Kevin Rudd as a possible replacement in the short term. It was reported that Gillard's supporter Bill Shorten was under pressure to ask her to resign, creating a vacancy that Rudd would contest.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="June_2013_leadership_vote">June 2013 leadership vote</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_June_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, June 2013">Australian Labor Party leadership spill, June 2013</a></div>
<p>By the end of June 2013, Labor's standing in the polls had worsened, and the Coalition had been leading in most opinion polls for two years; one poll in early June showed that Labor would be reduced to as few as 40 seats after the next election.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> With a general election due later that year, even some staunch Gillard supporters began to believe that Labor faced almost certain defeat if Gillard continued as leader. According to the ABC's <a href="/wiki/Barrie_Cassidy" title="Barrie Cassidy">Barrie Cassidy</a>, the question was not whether Gillard would be ousted as Labor leader, but when the ousting would take place.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following further speculation over her leadership, on 26 June a rumour emerged that supporters of Kevin Rudd were collecting signatures for a letter demanding an immediate leadership vote. That afternoon, before any letter had been published, Gillard called a leadership spill live on television.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> She challenged any would-be opponent to join her in a pledge that, while the winner would become leader, the loser would immediately retire from politics. Despite his earlier comments that he would not return to the leadership under any circumstances, Kevin Rudd announced that he would challenge Gillard for the leadership, and committed to retiring from politics if he lost. In the party-room ballot later that evening, Rudd defeated Gillard by a margin of 57 votes to 45.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Resignation_and_retirement_from_politics">Resignation and retirement from politics</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png/170px-Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png/255px-Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png/340px-Bronze_bust_of_PM_Julia_Gillard.png 2x" data-file-width="706" data-file-height="934" /></a><figcaption>Bronze bust of Julia Gillard at the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Avenue">Prime Minister's Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure><p>
Following her defeat in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_June_2013" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, June 2013">leadership vote</a> on 26 June 2013, Gillard congratulated Rudd on his win and announced that she would immediately tender her resignation as Prime Minister to the Governor-General, <a href="/wiki/Quentin_Bryce" title="Quentin Bryce">Quentin Bryce</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> She also announced, in keeping with her pledge before the leadership vote, that she would not re-contest her Labor seat at the upcoming election, and thus would retire from politics. In her final speech, she reflected on the honour of being the first female head of government in Australia and expressed confidence for the future of women leaders in Australia:<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There's been a lot of analysis about the so-called 'gender wars'. Me playing the so-called 'gender card' because heavens knows no-one noticed I was a woman until I raised it [...] I've been a little bit bemused by those colleagues in the newspapers who have admitted that I have suffered more pressure as a result of my gender than other prime ministers in the past but then concluded that it had zero effect on my political position or the political position of the Labor Party. It doesn't explain everything, it doesn't explain nothing, it explains some things. And it is for the nation to think in a sophisticated way about those shades of grey. What I am absolutely confident of is it will be easier for the next woman and the woman after that and the woman after that. And I'm proud of that.<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Gillard's resignation as Prime Minister took effect the following day, upon the swearing in of Rudd,<sup id="cite_ref-resignationletter_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-resignationletter-238">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> and she made her final appearance in the House of Representatives shortly thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Her parliamentary service ended at the dissolution of the Parliament on 5 August. By the conclusion of her tenure, Gillard overtook <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> as the <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia_by_time_in_office" title="List of prime ministers of Australia by time in office">15th longest–serving Prime Minister of Australia</a>, having served in the position for three full years.<sup id="cite_ref-Pmlengthchart_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pmlengthchart-241">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> She also became the longest–serving Prime Minister since John Howard's electoral loss in 2007, a record which was not exceeded until August 2021, when <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a> overtook her as the <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia_by_time_in_office" title="List of prime ministers of Australia by time in office">14th longest–serving Prime Minister of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Pmlengthchart_241-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pmlengthchart-241">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Subsequent to the federal election held on 7 September 2013, Gillard was succeeded as the Member for Lalor by her preferred replacement, <a href="/wiki/Joanne_Ryan_(politician)" title="Joanne Ryan (politician)">Joanne Ryan</a>, a former school principal.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_positions">Political positions</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_philosophy">Political philosophy</span></h3>
<p>Although nominally a member of the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Left" class="mw-redirect" title="Victorian Left">Victorian Left</a> faction of the Labor Party,<sup id="cite_ref-MaresBookReviewOfGillardBiography_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MaresBookReviewOfGillardBiography-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> her election to Prime Minister occurred because of support from the <a href="/wiki/Labor_Right" title="Labor Right">Labor Right</a> faction, with the <a href="/wiki/Labor_Left" title="Labor Left">Labor Left</a> planning to support Rudd in the Caucus vote had there actually been one.<sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> Analyses of <a href="/wiki/Jacqueline_Kent" title="Jacqueline Kent">Jacqueline Kent</a>'s 2009 biography of Gillard suggest that her membership in the Left faction is "more organisational than ideological".<sup id="cite_ref-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Book_review_of_Gillard_Biography-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MaresBookReviewOfGillardBiography_245-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MaresBookReviewOfGillardBiography-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> In July 2010, historian <a href="/wiki/Ross_Fitzgerald" title="Ross Fitzgerald">Ross Fitzgerald</a> said that "at least since [2009] Gillard has sought to reposition herself more towards the Labor Right."<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civil_liberties_and_democracy">Civil liberties and democracy</span></h3>
<p>Gillard supports Australia <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_Australia" title="Republicanism in Australia">becoming a republic</a> and has suggested that the end of Queen <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth&#160;II</a>'s reign would be "probably the appropriate point for a transition".<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> Following the elevation of republican <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a> to the prime-ministership in September 2015, Gillard along with Rudd tweeted their support for <a href="/wiki/Peter_FitzSimons" title="Peter FitzSimons">Peter FitzSimons</a>, the head of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Republican_Movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Republican Movement">Australian Republican Movement</a>, and his call for new members to join the movement.<sup id="cite_ref-gillardruddrepublic_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gillardruddrepublic-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the November 2010 release of secret <a href="/wiki/United_States_diplomatic_cables_leak" title="United States diplomatic cables leak">United States diplomatic cables</a>, Gillard stated, "I absolutely condemn the placement of this information on the <a href="/wiki/WikiLeaks" title="WikiLeaks">WikiLeaks</a> website. It's a grossly irresponsible thing to do and an illegal thing to do."<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> After an Australian Federal Police investigation failed to find WikiLeaks had broken any Australian laws by publishing the US diplomatic documents, Gillard maintained her stance that the release of the documents was "grossly irresponsible".<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_policy">Social policy</span></h3>
<p>Gillard expressed support for <a href="/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements" title="Abortion-rights movements">legal abortion</a> in 2005, saying that "Women without money would be left without that choice or in the hands of backyard abortion providers" and that she understood "the various moral positions" regarding abortions.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> Pertaining to unplanned pregnancies and counselling, Gillard is of the view that women ought to be couselled by someone of their choice – as opposed to only trained professionals referred to by their general practitioners.<sup id="cite_ref-abortioncounsel_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abortioncounsel-255">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> In August 2012, Gillard reiterated her position in support of abortion, stating that "Women must have the right to healthcare and women must have the right to choose."<sup id="cite_ref-abortion2cite_256-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abortion2cite-256">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to a 2012 report by think-tank Australia 21, which recommended the relaxation of <a href="/wiki/Illicit_drug_use_in_Australia" title="Illicit drug use in Australia">illicit drug laws in Australia</a>, Gillard rejected the report and claimed that "drugs kill people they rip families apart, they destroy lives&#160;... I am not in favour of decriminalisation of any of our drug laws."<sup id="cite_ref-drugs1_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-drugs1-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-drugs2_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-drugs2-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Concerning <a href="/wiki/Euthanasia" title="Euthanasia">euthanasia</a>, Gillard warned that it may "open the door to exploitation and perhaps callousness towards people in the end stage of life" and that she is not convinced that the policy of pro-euthanasia advocates contain "sufficient safeguards".<sup id="cite_ref-NoHope_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NoHope-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard, as a member of parliament, voted against a bill that would have legalised <a href="/wiki/Recognition_of_same-sex_unions_in_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Recognition of same-sex unions in Australia">same-sex marriage in Australia</a> in 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-Crowe201508_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crowe201508-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> In 2010 she stated "the Marriage Act is appropriate in its current form, that is recognising that marriage is between a man and a woman" and that marriage being between a man and woman "has a special status".<sup id="cite_ref-NoHope_259-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NoHope-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> The triennial Labor conference held in December 2011 saw Gillard successfully negotiate an amendment on same-sex marriage to see the party introduce a conscience vote to parliament through a private member's bill, rather than a binding vote.<sup id="cite_ref-tacticalswitch_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tacticalswitch-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> When the private members bill was introduced by Labor backbencher <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Jones_(Australian_politician)" title="Stephen Jones (Australian politician)">Stephen Jones</a>, it was defeated in the House of Representatives on 19 September 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-samesexdownabc_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-samesexdownabc-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> In September 2014, Gillard said that the "course of human history now is that we are going to see same-sex marriage here and in, you know, most parts of the developed world."<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> She declared her support for same-sex marriage in August 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-Crowe201508_260-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crowe201508-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-political_career_.282013.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-political_career_(2013–present)">Post-political career (2013–present)</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Publications_and_appearances">Publications and appearances</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales,_July_2015._Cropped.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png/170px-Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="192" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png/255px-Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png/340px-Julia_Gillard_delivers_keynote_speech_at_the_National_Assemby_for_Wales%2C_July_2015._Cropped.png 2x" data-file-width="1514" data-file-height="1714" /></a><figcaption>Gillard delivers a keynote address to the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_for_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly for Wales">National Assembly for Wales</a> on the representation of women in public life, in July 2015</figcaption></figure>
<p>In July 2013, Gillard signed a book deal for her memoirs with <a href="/wiki/Penguin_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Penguin Australia">Penguin Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> The autobiography, <i><a href="/wiki/My_Story_(Gillard_book)" title="My Story (Gillard book)">My Story</a></i>, was published in 2014 by <a href="/wiki/Random_House" title="Random House">Random House</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> In the book, Gillard reflects on various personal aspects of her life and career, including her own analysis of the people and key players during the Rudd–Gillard governments. Senator <a href="/wiki/Nick_Xenophon" title="Nick Xenophon">Nick Xenophon</a> was said to have been "infamously excluded from university for a period as punishment for stuffing a ballot box full of voting papers he had somehow procured", which was denied by Xenophon. In February 2015, Random House issued a public apology to Xenophon and paid a confidential cash settlement.<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> Following requests from Xenophon for a personal apology from Gillard, on 6 August 2015 she published a personal apology to him in a number of Australian newspapers.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following her departure from parliament at the 2013 election, Gillard has remained engaged with the Labor Party. After Labor's defeat at the federal election held in September 2013, Gillard penned an <a href="/wiki/Op-ed" title="Op-ed">op-ed</a> for <i><a href="/wiki/Guardian_Australia" title="Guardian Australia">Guardian Australia</a></i>, wherein she wrote about her legacy and how she believes the Labor Party ought to rebuild.<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> In June 2015, Gillard participated in <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Ferguson_(journalist)" title="Sarah Ferguson (journalist)">Sarah Ferguson</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/The_Killing_Season_(Australian_TV_series)" title="The Killing Season (Australian TV series)">The Killing Season</a></i>, a three-part documentary series which chronicles the events of the Rudd–Gillard years in power. The television series featured in-depth interviews with key Labor Party officials during the Rudd–Gillard governments.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> Prior to the <a href="/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_election" title="2016 Australian federal election">2016 election</a> campaign, Gillard offered her assistance to the Labor party, whereby a video was released of her endorsing and seeking donations for the party's education policy.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> She later joined former Labor Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> and <a href="/wiki/Paul_Keating" title="Paul Keating">Paul Keating</a> at Bill Shorten's Labor campaign launch on 19 June 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She had been a supporter of Hillary Clinton's <a href="/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_presidential_campaign,_2016" class="mw-redirect" title="Hillary Clinton presidential campaign, 2016">Democratic Party candidacy</a> for <a href="/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election" title="2016 United States presidential election">President of the United States</a>, from as early as September 2014 when Gillard announced that she would "loudly barrack from the sidelines" should Clinton run.<sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> Having endorsed Clinton after she announced her candidacy in April 2015,<sup id="cite_ref-274" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-274">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup> Gillard appeared in a campaign video in October, wherein she advocated for the presidential candidate and her leadership surrounding women's issues.<sup id="cite_ref-275" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-275">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-276" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-276">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> Gillard attended the first day of the <a href="/wiki/2016_Democratic_National_Convention" title="2016 Democratic National Convention">Democratic National Convention</a> in <a href="/wiki/Philadelphia" title="Philadelphia">Philadelphia</a> on 25 July 2016, alongside former US Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Madeleine_Albright" title="Madeleine Albright">Madeleine Albright</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> The following day, Gillard published an open letter to Clinton in <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Times" title="The New York Times">The New York Times</a></i>, urging voters to "shame sexism" levied against the Democratic presidential candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours_and_appointments">Honours and appointments</span></h3>
<p>In April 2014, Gillard was admitted to the degree of Doctor of <a href="/wiki/Victoria_University,_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria University, Australia">Victoria University</a>, <a href="/wiki/Honoris_causa" class="mw-redirect" title="Honoris causa">honoris causa</a>, for her accomplishments surrounding education and disability reform as a political leader.<sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup> On 11 February 2015, Gillard received an honorary doctorate from the <a href="/wiki/Vrije_Universiteit_Brussel" title="Vrije Universiteit Brussel">Vrije Universiteit Brussel</a> "for her achievements as a woman committed to education and to social inclusion, and for the impact of her commitment on the situation of children, youngsters and women worldwide";<sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> and she also held a Kapuscinski Development Lecture on "the importance of education in development contexts" at the said university.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> In October, she received an honorary doctorate from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Canberra" title="University of Canberra">University of Canberra</a>, for her work in "education and gender equality."<sup id="cite_ref-284" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-284">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> In January 2016 she opened the Julia Gillard Library in the Melbourne suburb of <a href="/wiki/Tarneit,_Victoria" title="Tarneit, Victoria">Tarneit</a>; the library's name was selected by the <a href="/wiki/City_of_Wyndham" title="City of Wyndham">Wyndham City Council</a> to recognise her contributions as both the local member of parliament and Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> Gillard was conferred an Honorary Doctorate of Laws by <a href="/wiki/Deakin_University" title="Deakin University">Deakin University</a>, for her promotion of "education opportunities in Australia, especially to groups under-represented in higher education", in December 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-287" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-287">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard,_July_2016.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg/220px-Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg/330px-Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg/440px-Julia_Gillard%2C_July_2016.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5760" data-file-height="3840" /></a><figcaption>Gillard at the Girls' Education Forum, held in London on 7 July 2016</figcaption></figure>
<p>Having moved back to Adelaide, Gillard was appointed an honorary Visiting Professor of Politics at the University of Adelaide in 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup> In October of that year, she joined the <a href="/wiki/Brookings_Institution" title="Brookings Institution">Brookings Institution</a>'s Center for Universal Education as a nonresident <a href="/wiki/Senior_fellow" class="mw-redirect" title="Senior fellow">senior fellow</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> In February 2014, Gillard was appointed chairwoman of the <a href="/wiki/Global_Partnership_for_Education" title="Global Partnership for Education">Global Partnership for Education</a>, an international organisation focused on getting all children into school for a quality education in the world's poorest countries.<sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, in December, Gillard joined the board of the mental health organisation <a href="/wiki/Beyond_Blue" title="Beyond Blue">Beyond Blue</a>, chaired by former Victorian Premier <a href="/wiki/Jeff_Kennett" title="Jeff Kennett">Jeff Kennett</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup> Kennett announced on 21 March 2017 that he would be stepping down from the position during the second–half of the year, almost 17 years after founding the organisation; Gillard succeeded him as chair of Beyond Blue on 1 July 2017, becoming the first former Prime Minister since <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser" title="Malcolm Fraser">Malcolm Fraser</a> to head a mental-health organisation.<sup id="cite_ref-294" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-294">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup> Since February 2015 she has been the patron of the John Curtin Prime Ministerial Library at <a href="/wiki/Curtin_University" title="Curtin University">Curtin University</a> in Perth.<sup id="cite_ref-295" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-295">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-296" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-296">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup> On 30 June 2015, she was conferred with a fellowship from <a href="/wiki/Aberystwyth_University" title="Aberystwyth University">Aberystwyth University</a> in recognition of her "significant contribution to political life".<sup id="cite_ref-297" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-297">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup> In September 2016 Gillard was appointed a visiting professor at <a href="/wiki/King%27s_College_London" title="King&#39;s College London">King's College London</a>, joining the King's Policy Institute to chair the Global Institute for Women's Leadership, as well as the Menzies Centre for Australian Studies.<sup id="cite_ref-298" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-298">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2017, Gillard was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a> (AC) "for eminent service to the Parliament of Australia, particularly as Prime Minister, through seminal contributions to economic and social development, particularly policy reform in the areas of education, disability care, workplace relations, health, foreign affairs and the environment, and as a role model to women."<sup id="cite_ref-299" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-299">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-300" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-300">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> According to <i><a href="/wiki/The_West_Australian" title="The West Australian">The West Australian</a></i>, one of her nominators for the award was then–Prime Minister Tony Abbott, who wrote a letter testifying to her suitability for the honour in 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> Gillard is the most recent former prime minister to have received such award since <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> in 2008, and the sixth prime minister overall.<sup id="cite_ref-302" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-302">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> In July 2017 she took up her appointment as chair of Beyond Blue.<sup id="cite_ref-303" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-303">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2018 she was listed as one of <a href="/wiki/100_Women_(BBC)" title="100 Women (BBC)">BBC's 100 Women</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-304" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-304">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2021, Gillard was appointed chair of the board of Governors at <a href="/wiki/Wellcome_Trust" title="Wellcome Trust">Wellcome Trust</a>, one of the most richly-endowed philanthropic charitable trusts, headquartered in London UK but with global reach, supporting research and innovation in medicine, public health, mental health and climate change.<sup id="cite_ref-wellcome1_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wellcome1-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-305" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-305">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup> In April 2021, Gillard was honoured by the award of the Grand Cordon of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Rising_Sun" title="Order of the Rising Sun">Order of the Rising Sun</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Japan" title="Government of Japan">Government of Japan</a>, which was formally presented by the <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Japan_to_Australia" title="List of ambassadors of Japan to Australia">Ambassador of Japan to Australia</a> in a ceremony held on 4 February 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-306" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-306">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-307" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-307">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup> Gillard is the 8th Australian prime minister to receive the award, after Edmund Barton, Robert Menzies, John McEwen, Gough Whitlam, Malcolm Fraser, Bob Hawke, and John Howard.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Royal_Commission_into_early_education_in_South_Australia">Royal Commission into early education in South Australia</span></h2>
<p>In October 2022 it was announced that Gillard would be leading the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission">Royal Commission</a> into early education in South Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-308" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-308">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg/170px-Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="198" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg/255px-Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg/340px-Julia_Gillard_and_Tim_Mathieson_January_2013_cropped.jpg 2x" data-file-width="871" data-file-height="1017" /></a><figcaption>Gillard with her partner, Tim Mathieson, in 2013</figcaption></figure>
<p>Gillard met <a href="/wiki/Tim_Mathieson" title="Tim Mathieson">Tim Mathieson</a> in 2004, and they began a relationship in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-wait_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wait-309">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-310" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-310">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> The relationship ended in 2020 or 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-311" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-311">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard's mother told ABC TV's <i>Australian Story</i> program that Gillard had spoken from a young age of never wanting children. Gillard herself told the program that while she admired women who could balance child rearing with a career, "I'm not sure I could have. There's something in me that's focused and single-minded and if I was going to do that, I'm not sure I could have done this."<sup id="cite_ref-312" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-312">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard owned a single-storey home in the south-western Melbourne suburb of Altona<sup id="cite_ref-Altona_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Altona-313">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> which she occupied prior to The Lodge and sold in December 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-314" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-314">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> She is a public supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Western_Bulldogs" title="Western Bulldogs">Western Bulldogs</a> <a href="/wiki/Australian_rules_football" title="Australian rules football">Australian rules football</a> team<sup id="cite_ref-315" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-315">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Storm" title="Melbourne Storm">Melbourne Storm</a> <a href="/wiki/National_Rugby_League" title="National Rugby League">rugby league</a> team.<sup id="cite_ref-316" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-316">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> She currently resides in Adelaide, in the beachside suburb of <a href="/wiki/Brighton,_South_Australia" title="Brighton, South Australia">Brighton</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard was brought up in the <a href="/wiki/Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptist">Baptist</a> tradition, but is an <a href="/wiki/Atheist" class="mw-redirect" title="Atheist">atheist</a>. In a 2010 interview, when asked if she believed in God, she stated: "No, I don't&#160;... I'm not a religious person&#160;... I'm a great respecter of religious beliefs but they're not my beliefs."<sup id="cite_ref-DoesNotBelieveInGod_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DoesNotBelieveInGod-318">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-319" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-319">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Comparing Australia to the United States in a 2013 interview with <i><a href="/wiki/The_Washington_Post" title="The Washington Post">The Washington Post</a></i>, she stated: "I think it would be inconceivable for me if I were an American to have turned up at the highest echelon of American politics being an atheist, single and childless."<sup id="cite_ref-320" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-320">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> In her 2014 autobiography, <i>My Story</i>, Gillard stated, "<a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin</a> had to be the leader in our alliance because I understood that I was not what Labor needed at that point: a woman, not married, an atheist."<sup id="cite_ref-321" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-321">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gillard has admitted to having smoked <a href="/wiki/Cannabis" title="Cannabis">cannabis</a> before, but said she "didn't like it".<sup id="cite_ref-322" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-322">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="AWU_affair">AWU affair</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/AWU_affair" title="AWU affair">AWU affair</a></div>
<p>Gillard worked in the industrial department of the law firm Slater &amp; Gordon from 1988 through to 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-Grech2012-08-20_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grech2012-08-20-323">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> In the early 1990s, she was in a relationship with Bruce Wilson, an official of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Workers%27_Union" title="Australian Workers&#39; Union">Australian Workers' Union</a> (AWU).<sup id="cite_ref-OurJulia_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OurJulia-324">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Timeline_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timeline-325">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Confession_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Confession-326">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup> Gillard provided <a href="/wiki/Pro-bono" class="mw-redirect" title="Pro-bono">pro-bono</a> legal assistance to help establish the AWU Workplace Reform Association for Wilson and his associate Ralph Blewitt.<sup id="cite_ref-327" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-327">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> She was also involved in providing legal services in relation to the purchase of a Fitzroy property by Wilson and Blewitt. Wilson and Blewitt have been accused of creating the association to use a <a href="/wiki/Slush_fund" title="Slush fund">slush fund</a> for personal benefit, including diverting funds for the purchase of the house in Fitzroy.<sup id="cite_ref-328" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-328">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Slater &amp; Gordon investigated Gillard's conduct and concluded that she had no case to answer.<sup id="cite_ref-Coorey1_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coorey1-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-NoEvidence_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NoEvidence-329">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Sales2012-11-22_330-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sales2012-11-22-330">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup> Gillard has denied any wrongdoing.<sup id="cite_ref-ClearTheAir_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ClearTheAir-331">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> A subsequent <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_trade_union_governance_and_corruption" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission into trade union governance and corruption">Royal Commission into union corruption</a> found that Gillard had not committed or known of any criminal activity, but had displayed a lapse in professional judgement.<sup id="cite_ref-332" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-332">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
